### Tool result

_source line 2337 · 2026-05-22T05:46:55.988149+00:00_

40	Our contributions are:
41	\begin{itemize}
42	    \item A bias decomposition identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) proving that global correction introduces bias proportional to $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$, with a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$) that flags when correction is harmful using only per-subgroup misclassification rates---independently actionable without EC-HTE (Sections~\ref{sec:theory},~\ref{sec:diagnostic}).
43	    \item EC-HTE: hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified confusion matrix estimation, compatible with any CATE estimator (CausalForestDML, R-learner) and composable with PPCI for dual covariate/outcome error ($|\text{bias}|$: $0.179 \to 0.007$), validated across 520 synthetic configurations, a semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$), and four open-source LLMs (7B--9B) confirming $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity on real annotations (Sections~\ref{sec:method}--\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).
44	    \item Applicability conditions and crossover analysis ($n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$ for $K{=}2$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$) establishing when EC-HTE improves over global correction and when it does not (Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}).
45	\end{itemize}

[Director 指令] [更新] Reviewer 验证完成，Civil Comments 写入策略微调：

**Reviewer 通过的内容（可以写入）：**
- 3 个 LLM 的 Δπ > 0.05 确认（子群定义合理，样本量足够）
- "validated across two NLP datasets and model families" 的 claim

**Reviewer 标记的注意事项（必须遵守）：**
1. **不引用 gpt-4o-mini/gpt-3.5-turbo 的 coverage 数据**——FPR≈0.5 导致 CI 宽到无信息量，"coverage 0.97" 是虚假校准。如果提 coverage，只用 gpt-4o 的数据。
2. **Δπ 聚合定义需明确**——Appendix 中说明 Δπ 的具体计算方式（max-min of per-subgroup rates）
3. 建议在 Appendix 注明 "subgroup features chosen a priori, no feature search performed"

**写入指令确认：**
- §5.5 加 1-2 句 Civil Comments 验证（之前已给的文字可以用，只报 Δπ，不报 coverage/RMSE）
- Appendix 新 section：数据集描述 + 混淆矩阵表 + Δπ 定义 + "a priori features" 声明
- 标注 gpt-4o-mini 和 gpt-3.5-turbo 属于 FPR≈0.5 failure mode（与 Limitations 一致）

现在可以 finalize 并编译。

_source line 2347 · 2026-05-22T05:48:24.144906+00:00_

756	\section{Civil Comments Validation}
757	\label{app:civilcomments}
758	
759	To verify that subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification generalizes beyond TweetEval, we annotate $N{=}3{,}000$ stratified samples (30\% toxic) from Civil Comments~\cite{borkan2019nuanced}, a toxicity detection dataset.
760	Subgroups are defined by \texttt{text\_length\_bin} $\times$ \texttt{has\_question} ($K{=}4$): S0 (long, no question, 38.8\%), S1 (long, question, 11.4\%), S2 (short, no question, 33.7\%), S3 (short, question, 16.1\%).
761	Three proprietary LLMs annotate each sample via an OpenAI-compatible API, yielding 800--900 valid annotations per model.
762	
763	Table~\ref{tab:civil_cm} reports per-subgroup FPR and FNR.
764	All three models exhibit $\Delta\pi > 0.05$, with overall heterogeneity $\Delta\pi \in [0.113, 0.160]$---comparable to the $\Delta\pi \in [0.125, 0.165]$ observed across four open-source LLMs on TweetEval (Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).
765	GPT-4o-mini and GPT-3.5-turbo show pronounced FPR/FNR asymmetry (e.g., GPT-4o-mini FPR $\in [0.42, 0.61]$ vs.\ FNR $\in [0.05, 0.16]$), indicating systematic over-prediction of toxicity.
766	The consistent presence of $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity across two NLP tasks, seven LLMs spanning open-source (7B--9B) and proprietary families, and two distinct subgroup definitions confirms that this phenomenon is a structural property of LLM annotation rather than an artifact of a specific dataset or model.
767	
768	\begin{table}[h]
769	\centering
770	\caption{Per-subgroup misclassification rates on Civil Comments ($K{=}4$). $\Delta$ denotes max $-$ min across subgroups.}
771	\label{tab:civil_cm}
772	\small
773	\begin{tabular}{llccccc}
774	\toprule
775	Model & Metric & S0 & S1 & S2 & S3 & $\Delta$ \\
776	\midrule
777	GPT-4o & FPR & 0.249 & 0.157 & 0.213 & 0.341 & 0.184 \\
778	       & FNR & 0.160 & 0.263 & 0.308 & 0.328 & 0.168 \\
779	       & $\Delta\pi$ & \multicolumn{5}{c}{0.160} \\
780	\midrule
781	GPT-4o-mini & FPR & 0.517 &

```json
  "old_string": "To verify that subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification generalizes beyond TweetEval, we annotate $N{=}3{,}000$ stratified samples (30\\% toxic) from Civil Comments~\\cite{borkan2019nuanced}, a toxicity detection dataset.\nSubgroups are defined by \\texttt{text\\_length\\_bin} $\\times$ \\texttt{has\\_question} ($K{=}4$): S0 (long, no question, 38.8\\%), S1 (long, question, 11.4\\%), S2 (short, no question, 33.7\\%), S3 (short, question, 16.1\\%).\nThree proprietary LLMs annotate each sample via an OpenAI-compatible API, yielding 800--900 valid annotations per model.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:civil_cm} reports per-subgroup FPR and FNR.\nAll three models exhibit $\\Delta\\pi > 0.05$, with overall heterogeneity $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0.113, 0.160]$---comparable to the $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0.125, 0.165]$ observed across four open-source LLMs on TweetEval (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).\nGPT-4o-mini and GPT-3.5-turbo show pronounced FPR/FNR asymmetry (e.g., GPT-4o-mini FPR $\\in [0.42, 0.61]$ vs.\\ FNR $\\in [0.05, 0.16]$), indicating systematic over-prediction of toxicity.\nThe consistent presence of $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity across two NLP tasks, seven LLMs spanning open-source (7B--9B) and proprietary families, and two distinct subgroup definitions confirms that this phenomenon is a structural property of LLM annotation rather than an artifact of a specific dataset or model.",
  "new_string": "To verify that subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification generalizes beyond TweetEval, we annotate $N{=}3{,}000$ stratified samples (30\\% toxic) from Civil Comments~\\cite{borkan2019nuanced}, a toxicity detection dataset.\nSubgroups are defined by \\texttt{text\\_length\\_bin} $\\times$ \\texttt{has\\_question} ($K{=}4$): S0 (long, no question, 38.8\\%), S1 (long, question, 11.4\\%), S2 (short, no question, 33.7\\%), S3 (short, question, 16.1\\%).\nSubgroup features were chosen a priori based on observable text properties; no feature search was performed.\nThree proprietary LLMs annotate each sample via an OpenAI-compatible API, yielding 800--900 valid annotations per model.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:civil_cm} reports per-subgroup FPR and FNR.\nWe define $\\Delta\\pi = \\max_s \\pi_s - \\min_s \\pi_s$, where $\\pi_s$ is the overall misclassification rate for subgroup $s$.\nAll three models exhibit $\\Delta\\pi > 0.05$, with overall heterogeneity $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0.113, 0.160]$---comparable to the $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0.125, 0.165]$ observed across four open-source LLMs on TweetEval (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).\nGPT-4o-mini and GPT-3.5-turbo show pronounced FPR/FNR asymmetry (e.g., GPT-4o-mini FPR $\\in [0.42, 0.61]$ vs.\\ FNR $\\in [0.05, 0.16]$), indicating systematic over-prediction of toxicity; their FPR approaches 0.5, placing them in the ill-conditioned regime where confusion matrix inversion amplifies noise (consistent with the classifier quality threshold discussed in Section~\\ref{sec:limitations}).\nThe consistent presence of $\\Delta\\pi$ heterogeneity across two NLP tasks, seven LLMs spanning open-source (7B--9B) and proprietary families, and two distinct subgroup definitions confirms that this phenomenon is a structural property of LLM annotation rather than an artifact of a specific dataset or model."
```

**⚠️ 空间预算警告**：以下修改会增加文字量，但页数必须 ≤ 8 页。每完成一组修改后编译验证页数，如果接近超限立刻压缩其他区域（§5.1 Setup 可从 17 行压到 12 行；Conclusion 可压到 3-4 句）。

---

**Tier 1: Must-Fix（先做）**

**C1 页数验证 + 预留空间**
先编译确认当前 Conclusion 位置。如果已在 page 8 以内，为后续修改预留空间：压缩 §5.1 Setup（17→12 行）和/或 Conclusion。

**W1 Appendix B 代数错误**
删除 appendix.tex lines 66-72（错误的第一条推导路径：(1−π̄)(1−π_s)−π̄π_s 展开错误）。只保留 "More directly" 路径（L75-86）作为唯一推导。这条路径已由 Reviewer 逐步验证为正确。

**W6 加 (A5) 假设**
Section 3 Theorem 1 前增加：
- (A5): τ(z,s) = τ(z) for all s
- Remark（紧跟 Theorem 1 后 1-2 句）："Assumption (A5) simplifies exposition; the identity generalizes to subgroup-specific Δτ_s when treatment effects vary across subgroups (see Appendix [hetero-τ section])."

---

**Tier 2: Should-Fix**

**W2 Abstract 平衡**
在 Abstract L4 的 "|bias| from 0.059 to 0.010" 后加注 "(K=2, extreme Δπ)"。
**不要写 "balanced subgroups"**（Table 1 K=4 就是 unbalanced 39/38/12/11%）。
当前 Abstract L7 的 conditional qualifier 保留不动。

**W3 §5.5 联合假设违反**
§5.5 加 1-2 句："These experiments implicitly violate both (A2) (asymmetric errors: FPR≠FNR) and (A3) (class imbalance: P(Z=1)≈0.65). EC-HTE maintains coverage ≥0.93 at K=2, n_expert=1,000 under this joint violation, demonstrating robustness beyond the symmetric-balanced setting of Theorem 1."

**W4 计量经济学文献**
Appendix F 加 1 段（≤300 words）：
- Frazis & Loewenstein (2003), Bound et al. (2001), Black et al. (2000): ATE bias from binary misclassification, no HTE
- Mahajan (2006), Lewbel (2007): IV-based identification, EC-HTE doesn't need IV
- 我们的区分：subgroup-level HTE + heterogeneous misclassification + product amplification
- 5 个新 bib 条目（引用格式需验证）

**W5 "50%" 补充条件统计**
§5.2 中 "50% of configurations" 后加："Among subgroups with below-average misclassification (π_s < π̄), 84.6% experience increased bias under global correction."

---

**Tier 3: Nice-to-Fix（空间允许时做）**

**W7 统计显著性**
Abstract 中 bias reduction claim 处加："(significant at α=0.05 in 72.7% of 1,020 pairwise comparisons; >95% under extreme heterogeneity)"

**W8 κ>100 阈值**
§4 或 Appendix 加 1-2 句："For a 2×2 symmetric confusion matrix, κ = 1/|1−2π|; κ>100 corresponds to π ∈ (0.495, 0.505), triggering the fallback only when the classifier performs within 0.5% of random guessing."

**W9 诊断 FP/FN 代价**
§5.4 recommended workflow 后加 2 句："False negatives (failing to flag harmful correction) carry higher cost—potentially reversing CATE signs—than false positives (unnecessarily applying EC-HTE, which adds variance without systematic directional error). Practitioners should therefore prefer a conservative threshold."

**m1 数值例子**
Theorem 1 前加 2-3 句 inline example："For instance, if π₁=0.05 and π₂=0.25 (π̄=0.15), then δ₁=−0.10 and the bias for subgroup 1 equals −0.10·Δτ/(1−0.30)=−0.143Δτ: global correction overcorrects the low-error subgroup by 14.3% of the treatment effect gap."

**m4 "debiasing" 定义**
首次使用时加："(correction of measurement-error bias in causal estimates, distinct from algorithmic fairness debiasing)"

**m7 M_s = C_s 条件**
在等式处加 "under (A2) and (A3)"。

58	\end{equation}
59	Multiplying by $M_s$:
60	\begin{equation}
61	\resizebox{.88\columnwidth}{!}{$\displaystyle
62	M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s = \frac{1}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \begin{bmatrix} (1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\pi_s) - \bar{\pi}\pi_s & (1{-}\bar{\pi})\pi_s - \bar{\pi}(1{-}\pi_s) \\ -\bar{\pi}(1{-}\pi_s) + (1{-}\bar{\pi})\pi_s & -\bar{\pi}\pi_s + (1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\pi_s) \end{bmatrix}.
63	$}
64	\end{equation}
65	
66	Define $\delta_s = \pi_s - \bar{\pi}$. The $(1,1)$ entry simplifies:
67	{\small
68	\begin{align}
69	(1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\pi_s) - \bar{\pi}\pi_s &= 1 - \bar{\pi} - \pi_s + 2\bar{\pi}\pi_s \nonumber\\
70	&= (1 - 2\bar{\pi}) + (\bar{\pi} - \pi_s) + 2\bar{\pi}\pi_s \nonumber\\
71	&= (1 - 2\bar{\pi}) - \delta_s.
72	\end{align}
73	}
74	
75	More directly, substituting $\pi_s = \bar{\pi} + \delta_s$:
76	\begin{equation}
77	M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s - I = \frac{\delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \begin{bmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 1 & -1 \end{bmatrix}.
78	\end{equation}
79	
80	To verify: the $(1,1)$ entry of $M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s$ is
81	{\small
82	\begin{align}
83	&\frac{(1{-}\bar{\pi})(1{-}\bar{\pi}{-}\delta_s) - \bar{\pi}(\bar{\pi}{+}\delta_s)}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \nonumber\\
84	&= \frac{(1{-}\bar{\pi})^2 - (1{-}\bar{\pi})\delta_s - \bar{\pi}^2 - \bar{\pi}\delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}} \nonumber\\
85	&= \frac{1 - 2\bar{\pi} - \delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}} = 1 - \frac{\delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}}.
86	\end{align}
87	}
88	
89	Applying $(M_{\text{global}}^{-1} M_s - I)$ to $\boldsymbol{\tau}$:
90	{\small
91	\begin{align}
92	\text{bias}(z{=}0, s) &= \tfrac{\delta_s}{1-2\bar{\pi}}(-\tau(0) + \tau(1)) = +\tfrac{\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau}{1-2\bar{\pi}}, \\

0	\section{Experiments}
1	
2	\subsection{Setup}
3	
4	We evaluate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data generating processes (DGPs); full specifications appear in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
5	The synthetic DGP generates $N{=}5{,}000$ observations from a randomized trial with $K \in \{2, 4\}$ subgroups, controlled CATE heterogeneity $\Delta\tau$, and subgroup-specific symmetric misclassification rates with heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$.
6	Expert labels ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{50, \ldots, 2000\}$) are a simple random sample with true $Z$ observed; each configuration is repeated for 100 Monte Carlo runs.
7	The semi-synthetic DGP samples $N{=}5{,}000$ tweets from TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, defining $K{=}4$ subgroups by crossing hashtag presence and text length (unbalanced: 39\%/38\%/12\%/11\%), with misclassification rates $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$ reflecting empirical evidence that text features degrade LLM accuracy~\cite{ashwin2025bias}.
8	
9	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup MLE without pooling), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
10	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
11	Our Global baseline is equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} applied with a pooled (homogeneous) error rate; Stratified MLE corresponds to per-subgroup DSL without hierarchical shrinkage.
12	EC-HTE extends this by borrowing strength across subgroups via hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
13	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
14	Real-data validation on hate speech detection confirms $\Delta\pi = 0.117$ across text-feature subgroups (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
15	The heterogeneity pattern persists across six OpenAI models spanning three generations (Appen

6	\section{Bias Decomposition: When Debiasing Hurts}
7	\label{sec:theory}
8	
9	We formalize the bias that global misclassification correction introduces when subgroup error rates are heterogeneous.
10	Consider estimating the conditional average treatment effect (CATE) $\tau(z) = \mathbb{E}[Y(1) - Y(0) \mid Z = z]$, where $Z \in \{0, 1\}$ is a binary effect modifier observed only through a noisy annotation $\hat{Z}$.
11	The population consists of $K$ subgroups $S \in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ with weights $w_s = P(S{=}s)$, each characterized by a $2 \times 2$ confusion matrix $C_s$ with symmetric error rate $\pi_s = P(\hat{Z} \neq Z \mid S{=}s)$.
12	Standard practice estimates a single confusion matrix $\bar{C} = \sum_s w_s C_s$ from expert annotations and applies its inverse uniformly to all observations, with pooled error rate $\bar{\pi} = \sum_s w_s \pi_s$.
13	We show that when error rates vary across subgroups, this global procedure introduces a bias with an exact product form.
14	
15	We state four assumptions maintained throughout.
16	%
17	\begin{itemize}
18	\item[\textbf{(A1)}] \textit{Conditional independence:} misclassification depends only on the true label $Z$ and subgroup $S$, not on potential outcomes $Y(0), Y(1)$.
19	\item[\textbf{(A2)}] \textit{Symmetric misclassification:} $P(\hat{Z}{=}1 \mid Z{=}0, S{=}s) = P(\hat{Z}{=}0 \mid Z{=}1, S{=}s) = \pi_s$ for all $s$.
20	\item[\textbf{(A3)}] \textit{Balanced effect modifier:} $P(Z{=}0) = P(Z{=}1) = 0.5$. This simplifies the exposition; we discuss relaxation below.
21	\item[\textbf{(A4)}] \textit{Non-degeneracy:} $\pi_s \neq 0.5$ for all $s$, ensuring each $C_s$ is invertible.
22	\end{itemize}
23	
24	Under (A2)--(A3), the Bayesian mixing matrix $M_s$ (entries $M_s[\hat{z}, z] = P(Z{=}z \mid \hat{Z}{=}\hat{z}, S{
…[truncated 6276 chars]

487:\paragraph{Heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP.}
488:To assess whether the constant-$\tau$ DGP understates EC-HTE's advantage, we run a supplementary experiment using Llama's empirical confusion matrices with heterogeneous treatment effects across subgroups ($\Delta\tau \in [0.1, 0.6]$, $K{=}2$, 50 MC runs).
489-EC-HTE achieves lower $|\text{bias}|$ than global correction at all budgets ($n{=}500$: 0.010 vs.\ 0.031, a 68\% reduction).

```json
  "old_string": "We compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup MLE without pooling), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nOur Global baseline is equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} applied with a pooled (homogeneous) error rate; Stratified MLE corresponds to per-subgroup DSL without hierarchical shrinkage.\nEC-HTE extends this by borrowing strength across subgroups via hierarchical Bayesian estimation.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nReal-data validation on hate speech detection confirms $\\Delta\\pi = 0.117$ across text-feature subgroups (Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}).\nThe heterogeneity pattern persists across six OpenAI models spanning three generations (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm}) and four open-source LLMs in the 7B--9B parameter range (Section~\\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).\nEC-HTE maintains $\\geq$94.5\\% coverage under up to 50\\% subgroup misspecification noise (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\nWhen empirical asymmetric confusion matrices from the GPT-4.1 pilot are used directly, a subgroup with FNR~$\\approx 0.5$ causes EC-HTE to underperform global correction at all budgets (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal}).",
  "new_string": "We compare: (1) \\textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \\textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \\textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \\textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \\textit{PPCI}~\\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \\textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.\nFor CausalForest~\\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.\nPrimary metrics are $|\\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.\nRobustness validation covers hate speech detection ($\\Delta\\pi{=}0.117$; Appendix~\\ref{app:hatespeech}), six OpenAI models (Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm}), subgroup misspecification ($\\geq$94.5\\% coverage at 50\\% noise; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), and asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\\ref{app:semireal})."
}
```

94	
95	\subsection{Open-Source Multi-LLM Validation}
96	\label{sec:multilm_opensource}
97	
98	To verify that misclassification heterogeneity and EC-HTE's correction generalize beyond proprietary APIs, we annotate 500 TweetEval sentiment tweets with four open-source LLMs: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Gemma-2-9B-IT.
99	Each model independently classifies tweets using a 3-class prompt (positive/neutral/negative); we define a balanced binary label $Z{=}1$ if the true label $\geq 1$ (neutral or positive vs.\ negative), yielding $P(Z{=}1) \approx 0.65$.
100	Per-subgroup confusion matrices are estimated directly from each model's annotations against gold labels.
101	The EC-HTE pipeline---HB confusion matrix estimation followed by CausalForestDML---is identical to the synthetic experiments in Table~\ref{tab:main_results}.
102	All four models exhibit subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification ($\Delta\pi \in [0.125, 0.165]$, $K{=}4$), with consistent subgroup error rankings across architectures (Table~\ref{tab:multilm_cm}).
103	Correction improves 95\% CI coverage from 0.714 (naive average) to 0.939 ($+$22.5 percentage points, $K{=}2$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$), restoring near-nominal inference across all four models.
104	Under the moderate $\Delta\pi$ observed in these real LLM annotations, global correction suffices for RMSE.
105	Stratified correction achieves lower RMSE than the naive estimator only at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 1{,}000$ ($K{=}2$); global correction achieves comparable or lower RMSE than HB EC-HTE at all budgets under this moderate heterogeneity, consistent with the budget guidelines in Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}.
106	We replicate this finding on Civil Comments~\cite{borkan2019nuanced} (toxicity detection, $N{=}3{,}000$): three proprietary LLMs (GPT-4o, GPT-4o-mini, GPT-3.5-turbo) exhibit $\Delta\pi \in [0.113, 0.160]$ across text-feature subgroups ($K{=}4$), confirming that subgroup-heterogeneous misclassificat

_source line 2409 · 2026-05-22T06:26:04.201184+00:00_

18	The bias decomposition identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) predicts that global correction introduces bias proportional to $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ at the subgroup level.
19	We verify this identity empirically across 520 configurations spanning $K \in \{2,4\}$, two misclassification regimes, and six expert budget levels.
20	Regressing observed subgroup bias on the predicted $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ product yields $R^2 = 0.994$ for $K{=}2$ and $R^2 = 0.974$ for $K{=}4$; the residual variance reflects MC sampling noise rather than model misspecification, since Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} is an exact identity at the population level ($R^2 \geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$; Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}).
21	In 50\% of configurations (260/520, excluding $\Delta_c{=}0$ rows where bias is zero by construction), global correction produces larger absolute bias than the naive estimator.
22	The damage concentrates in low-$\pi$ subgroups: among subgroups where $\pi_s < \bar{\pi}$, global correction increases bias in 84.6\% of cases.
23	Maximum observed amplification reaches 2.86$\times$ (K=2) and 2.41$\times$ (K=4) relative to the naive estimator.
24	Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} visualizes the $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ amplification surface across the parameter grid.
25	
26	\begin{figure*}[t]
27	\centering
28	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig2_heatmap.pdf}
29	\caption{Bias amplification under global correction across the $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ parameter grid ($K{=}2$). (a) Low-error subgroup: global correction always helps (ratio $< 1$). (b) High-error subgroup: when $\Delta\pi > 0.12$ (dashed line), global correction \emph{increases} bias relative to the naive estimator (hatched region), with amplification reaching 2.86$\times$.}

181	\section{Extended Related Work}
182	\label{app:related}
183	
184	\paragraph{Measurement error details.}
185	\citet{fuller1987measurement} establish the foundational theory showing that naive regression with mismeasured covariates yields attenuated coefficient estimates.
186	\citet{carroll2006measurement} extend this to nonlinear models.
187	\citet{gustafson2003measurement} develops Bayesian treatments modeling measurement jointly with outcomes.
188	\citet{hu2008nonparametric} provide nonparametric identification using repeated measurements or instruments.
189	
190	\paragraph{Annotation model extensions.}
191	Beyond the foundational \citet{dawid1979maximum} model, \citet{passonneau2014benefits} incorporate item difficulty, \citet{hovy2013learning} learn annotator competence, and \citet{paun2018comparing} introduce hierarchical priors sharing information across annotators with varying expertise.
192	
193	\paragraph{PPI extensions and text-as-data methods.}
194	\citet{zrnic2024cross} extend PPI via cross-prediction; their active inference framework~\citep{zrnic2024active} achieves $>$80\% expert budget savings through adaptive allocation, complementary to our fixed-budget analysis.
195	\citet{grimmer2022text} and \citet{audinet2025benchmarking} provide broader methodological guidance for text-as-data pipelines without specific correction for causal heterogeneity.
196	
197	\paragraph{Treatment vs.\ effect-modifier misclassification.}
198	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification where errors change which arm a unit is assigned to, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification because the fundamental treated-vs-control contrast is corrupted.
199	Our problem preserves the treatment contrast ($T$ is observed correctly) but corrupts the heterogeneity structure ($\hat{Z} \neq Z$).
200	The ATE remains identified; only conditional effects are biased, which makes exact correction through confusion matrix inversion feasible.
201	
202	\section{MCMC

```json
  "old_string": "Our problem preserves the treatment contrast ($T$ is observed correctly) but corrupts the heterogeneity structure ($\\hat{Z} \\neq Z$).\nThe ATE remains identified; only conditional effects are biased, which makes exact correction through confusion matrix inversion feasible.\n\n\\section{MCMC Convergence Diagnostics}",
  "new_string": "Our problem preserves the treatment contrast ($T$ is observed correctly) but corrupts the heterogeneity structure ($\\hat{Z} \\neq Z$).\nThe ATE remains identified; only conditional effects are biased, which makes exact correction through confusion matrix inversion feasible.\n\n\\paragraph{Econometrics of binary misclassification.}\n\\citet{bound2001measurement} survey measurement error in survey data, establishing that binary misclassification attenuates regression coefficients.\n\\citet{frazis2003estimating} derive exact bias formulas for OLS with a misclassified binary regressor, showing attenuation proportional to the misclassification rate---analogous to our Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} but restricted to average effects without subgroup heterogeneity.\n\\citet{black2000racial} demonstrate that racial misclassification in administrative data can substantially bias estimates of racial disparities.\n\\citet{mahajan2006identification} and \\citet{lewbel2007estimation} develop instrumental variable approaches to identify models with misclassified binary regressors, requiring exclusion restrictions unavailable when the mismeasured variable is an LLM annotation.\nOur setting differs in three respects: (i) the mismeasured variable is an effect modifier indexing the CATE function, not a standard regressor; (ii) misclassification rates vary across subgroups, creating the $\\delta_s \\times \\Delta\\tau$ product amplification absent from homogeneous-error analyses; and (iii) expert labels provide a direct calibration sample, enabling confusion matrix estimation without instruments.\n\n\\section{MCMC Convergence Diagnostics}"
}
```

</details>

_source line 2433 · 2026-05-22T06:28:19.127640+00:00_

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We proved an exact bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\% of configurations.
4	EC-HTE addresses this via hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified correction with a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$); composed with PPCI, it reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007.
5	The workflow requires only a pilot sample: check $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$ and apply EC-HTE ($n_{\text{expert}} \geq 250$ for $K{=}2$, $\geq 1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$) when triggered; otherwise global correction suffices.
6	EC-HTE's advantage is conditional---the coverage gain (0.71 $\to$ 0.94) matters because undercovering intervals produce false confidence in spurious effects, but when $\Delta\pi$ is small, global correction remains appropriate.

95	\subsection{Open-Source Multi-LLM Validation}
96	\label{sec:multilm_opensource}
97	
98	To verify that misclassification heterogeneity and EC-HTE's correction generalize beyond proprietary APIs, we annotate 500 TweetEval sentiment tweets with four open-source LLMs: Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Gemma-2-9B-IT.
99	Each model independently classifies tweets using a 3-class prompt (positive/neutral/negative); we define a balanced binary label $Z{=}1$ if the true label $\geq 1$ (neutral or positive vs.\ negative), yielding $P(Z{=}1) \approx 0.65$.
100	Per-subgroup confusion matrices are estimated directly from each model's annotations against gold labels.
101	The EC-HTE pipeline---HB confusion matrix estimation followed by CausalForestDML---is identical to the synthetic experiments in Table~\ref{tab:main_results}.
102	All four models exhibit subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification ($\Delta\pi \in [0.125, 0.165]$, $K{=}4$), with consistent subgroup error rankings across architectures (Table~\ref{tab:multilm_cm}).
103	Correction improves 95\% CI coverage from 0.714 (naive average) to 0.939 ($+$22.5 percentage points, $K{=}2$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$), restoring near-nominal inference across all four models.
104	Under the moderate $\Delta\pi$ observed in these real LLM annotations, global correction suffices for RMSE.
105	Stratified correction achieves lower RMSE than the naive estimator only at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 1{,}000$ ($K{=}2$); global correction achieves comparable or lower RMSE than HB EC-HTE at all budgets under this moderate heterogeneity, consistent with the budget guidelines in Appendix~\ref{sec:budget}.
106	These experiments implicitly violate both (A2) (asymmetric errors: $\text{FPR} \neq \text{FNR}$) and (A3) (class imbalance: $P(Z{=}1) \approx 0.65$).
107	EC-HTE maintains coverage $\geq$0.93 at $K{=}2$, $n_{\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$ under this joint violation, demonstrating robustness beyond the symmet

```json
  "old_string": "Correction improves 95\\% CI coverage from 0.714 (naive average) to 0.939 ($+$22.5 percentage points, $K{=}2$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$), restoring near-nominal inference across all four models.\nUnder the moderate $\\Delta\\pi$ observed in these real LLM annotations, global correction suffices for RMSE.\nStratified correction achieves lower RMSE than the naive estimator only at $n_{\\text{expert}} \\geq 1{,}000$ ($K{=}2$); global correction achieves comparable or lower RMSE than HB EC-HTE at all budgets under this moderate heterogeneity, consistent with the budget guidelines in Appendix~\\ref{sec:budget}.\nThese experiments implicitly violate both (A2) (asymmetric errors: $\\text{FPR} \\neq \\text{FNR}$) and (A3) (class imbalance: $P(Z{=}1) \\approx 0.65$).\nEC-HTE maintains coverage $\\geq$0.93 at $K{=}2$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}1{,}000$ under this joint violation, demonstrating robustness beyond the symmetric-balanced setting of Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}.",
```

_source line 2459 · 2026-05-22T06:31:11.083047+00:00_

12	Standard practice estimates a single confusion matrix $\bar{C} = \sum_s w_s C_s$ from expert annotations and applies its inverse uniformly to all observations, with pooled error rate $\bar{\pi} = \sum_s w_s \pi_s$.
13	We show that when error rates vary across subgroups, this global procedure introduces a bias with an exact product form.
14	
15	We state four assumptions maintained throughout.
16	%
17	\begin{itemize}
18	\item[\textbf{(A1)}] \textit{Conditional independence:} misclassification depends only on the true label $Z$ and subgroup $S$, not on potential outcomes $Y(0), Y(1)$.
19	\item[\textbf{(A2)}] \textit{Symmetric misclassification:} $P(\hat{Z}{=}1 \mid Z{=}0, S{=}s) = P(\hat{Z}{=}0 \mid Z{=}1, S{=}s) = \pi_s$ for all $s$.
20	\item[\textbf{(A3)}] \textit{Balanced effect modifier:} $P(Z{=}0) = P(Z{=}1) = 0.5$. This simplifies the exposition; we discuss relaxation below.
21	\item[\textbf{(A4)}] \textit{Non-degeneracy:} $\pi_s \neq 0.5$ for all $s$, ensuring each $C_s$ is invertible.
22	\item[\textbf{(A5)}] \textit{Homogeneous treatment effects across subgroups:} $\tau(z, s) = \tau(z)$ for all $s$, so $\Delta\tau = \tau(1) - \tau(0)$ is a scalar.
23	\end{itemize}
24	
25	Under (A2)--(A3), the Bayesian mixing matrix $M_s$ (entries $M_s[\hat{z}, z] = P(Z{=}z \mid \hat{Z}{=}\hat{z}, S{=}s)$) coincides with $C_s$.
26	The global mixing matrix is $M_{\text{global}} = \sum_s w_s M_s$.
27	
28	\begin{theorem}[Bias Decomposition Identity]
29	\label{thm:bias_identity}

30-In practice, Laplace smoothing of the confusion matrix counts ensures finite-sample invertibility.
31:We impose a singularity guard: if the condition number $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$, subgroup $s$ falls back to global correction $\bar{C}^{-1}$ rather than risk numerical instability from near-singular inversion.
32-
--
49-\paragraph{Diagnostic criterion.}
50:Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction harms subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, a condition depending only on per-subgroup misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample.
51-This breaks the circular dependency that would arise if the diagnostic required CATE estimates.
--
55-EC-HTE corrects structured covariate misclassification through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ inversion but cannot address outcome-level measurement error.
56:PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} corrects residual outcome bias using a debiasing estimator but assumes correct covariate labels.
57-The two methods are complementary: apply EC-HTE first to correct covariate labels, then PPCI to correct outcome predictions.

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Large language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.
5	Yet these annotations are not uniformly accurate.
6	\citet{ashwin2025bias} document that LLM misclassification rates vary by up to 5$\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level.
7	In a pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\% binary accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\% to 20.0\% across text-feature subgroups ($\Delta\pi = 0.098$), within the range assumed in our synthetic DGPs ($\Delta\pi = 0.05$--$0.20$).
8	When such annotations serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, subgroup-specific errors propagate directly into conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.
9	The resulting bias is not random noise that averages out; it is structured by the same demographic axes that define the research question.
10	Standard correction procedures, which estimate a single confusion matrix and apply its inverse uniformly, cannot account for this heterogeneity.
11	
12	We formalize this problem and discover a previously unrecognized bias mechanism.
13	When a pooled confusion matrix is applied to correct subgroup CATEs, it misattributes error across subgroups with an exact product form: the bias in subgroup $s$ equals $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1 - 2\bar{\pi})$, where $\delta_s$ is the deviation from the pooled error rate and $\Delta\tau$ is the CATE heterogeneity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}).
14	This is not a bound but an exact population-level identity, verified empirically with $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $R^2 = 0.974$ ($K{=}4$).
15	A direct consequence is that global correction can be worse than no correction at all:
…[truncated 5145 chars]

6	\section{Bias Decomposition: When Debiasing Hurts}
7	\label{sec:theory}
8	
9	We formalize the bias that global misclassification correction\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.} introduces when subgroup error rates are heterogeneous.
10	Consider estimating the conditional average treatment effect (CATE) $\tau(z) = \mathbb{E}[Y(1) - Y(0) \mid Z = z]$, where $Z \in \{0, 1\}$ is a binary effect modifier observed only through a noisy annotation $\hat{Z}$.
11	The population consists of $K$ subgroups $S \in \{1, \ldots, K\}$ with weights $w_s = P(S{=}s)$, each characterized by a $2 \times 2$ confusion matrix $C_s$ with symmetric error rate $\pi_s = P(\hat{Z} \neq Z \mid S{=}s)$.
12	Standard practice estimates a single confusion matrix $\bar{C} = \sum_s w_s C_s$ from expert annotations and applies its inverse uniformly to all observations, with pooled error rate $\bar{\pi} = \sum_s w_s \pi_s$.
13	We show that when error rates vary across subgroups, this global procedure introduces a bias with an exact product form.
14	
15	We state four assumptions maintained throughout.
16	%
17	\begin{itemize}
18	\item[\textbf{(A1)}] \textit{Conditional independence:} misclassification depends only on the true label $Z$ and subgroup $S$, not on potential outcomes $Y(0), Y(1)$.
19	\item[\textbf{(A2)}] \textit{Symmetric misclassification:} $P(\hat{Z}{=}1 \mid Z{=}0, S{=}s) = P(\hat{Z}{=}0 \mid Z{=}1, S{=}s) = \pi_s$ for all $s$.
20	\item[\textbf{(A3)}] \textit{Balanced effect modifier:} $P(Z{=}0) = P(Z{=}1) = 0.5$. This simplifies the exposition; we discuss relaxation below.
21	\item[\textbf{(A4)}] \textit{Non-degeneracy:} $\pi_s \neq 0.5$ for all $s$, ensuring each $C_s$ is invertible.

1	\section{EC-HTE: Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	EC-HTE corrects subgroup-level misclassification bias in three stages: estimate per-subgroup confusion matrices via hierarchical Bayes, invert them to correct observed outcome moments, and propagate correction uncertainty into downstream causal estimators.
5	The complete procedure is summarized in Algorithm~\ref{alg:echte} (Appendix~\ref{app:algorithm}).
6	
7	\subsection{Hierarchical Bayes Confusion Matrix Estimation}
8	
9	We place a Dirichlet-Multinomial hierarchical model over the confusion matrices $\{C_s\}_{s=1}^K$.
10	For each subgroup $s$, row $j$ of $C_s$ has a Dirichlet prior $\text{Dir}(\alpha_0 \cdot \mathbf{e}_j)$, where $\mathbf{e}_j$ is the $j$-th standard basis vector and $\alpha_0 > 0$ is a shared concentration parameter.
11	A $\text{Gamma}(3, 1)$ hyperprior on $\alpha_0$ controls the strength of partial pooling across subgroups: large $\alpha_0$ pulls subgroup estimates toward the identity matrix (low error), while small $\alpha_0$ allows subgroup-specific rates to dominate.
12	This partial pooling implements a James-Stein shrinkage effect, borrowing strength across subgroups to reduce variance for small subgroups while preserving heterogeneity where the data support it.
13	
14	Posterior inference uses a Gibbs sampler with 4 chains, 4000 iterations (2000 warmup).
15	We require convergence diagnostics $\hat{R} < 1.01$ and effective sample size $\text{ESS} > 845$ for all parameters before accepting estimates.
16	In practice, all runs achieve $\hat{R} < 1.0034$ and $\text{ESS} > 1618$, well within these thresholds.
17	HB estimation outperforms stratified MLE in 87.5\% of configurations (21 of 24) across the experimental grid (Section~\ref{sec:ec_hte_eval}), with the largest gains when $n_{\text{expert}} / K < 100$.
18	The advantage requires at least $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup; below this threshold, stratified MLE 
…[truncated 4126 chars]

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}
5	Classical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.
6	We formalize this principle for a specific causal estimand: Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} gives an exact identity for CATE bias under global correction with heterogeneous misclassification.
7	\citet{ogburn2012nondifferential} show nondifferential confounder misclassification attenuates effects; our setting differs---the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier, and correction can amplify bias.
8	\citet{battistin2014treatment} study treatment effect estimation with covariate measurement error but focus on average effects rather than subgroup-level heterogeneity; \citet{fong2021machine} analyze error propagation when ML predictions serve as regression covariates but do not address the effect modifier setting.
9	In the annotation literature, \citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical priors~\cite{passonneau2014benefits,hovy2013learning,paun2018comparing}.
10	Our hierarchical estimator (Section~\ref{sec:method}) adapts this architecture from label aggregation to causal inference, tying the prior structure to the subgroup dimension that defines treatment effect heterogeneity.
11	
12	\paragraph{HTE estimation and LLM annotation error.}
13	Causal forests~\cite{athey2016recursive,wager2018causal}, double/debiased ML~\cite{chernozhukov2018dml}, doubly-robust CATE estimators~\cite{kennedy2023dr}, and meta-learners~\cite{kun
…[truncated 2965 chars]

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Setup}
4	
5	We evaluate on synthetic and semi-synthetic data generating processes (DGPs); full specifications appear in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
6	The synthetic DGP generates $N{=}5{,}000$ observations from a randomized trial with $K \in \{2, 4\}$ subgroups, controlled CATE heterogeneity $\Delta\tau$, and subgroup-specific symmetric misclassification rates with heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$.
7	Expert labels ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{50, \ldots, 2000\}$) are a simple random sample with true $Z$ observed; each configuration is repeated for 100 Monte Carlo runs.
8	The semi-synthetic DGP samples $N{=}5{,}000$ tweets from TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, defining $K{=}4$ subgroups by crossing hashtag presence and text length (unbalanced: 39\%/38\%/12\%/11\%), with misclassification rates $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$ reflecting empirical evidence that text features degrade LLM accuracy~\cite{ashwin2025bias}.
9	
10	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
11	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
12	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
13	Robustness validation covers hate speech detection ($\Delta\pi{=}0.117$; Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}), six OpenAI models (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm}), subgroup misspecification ($\geq$94.5\% coverage at 50\% noise; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}), and asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}).
14

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We proved an exact bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\% of configurations.
4	EC-HTE addresses this via hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified correction with a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$); composed with PPCI, it reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007.
5	EC-HTE's advantage requires sufficient $\Delta\pi$; when heterogeneity is small, global correction suffices ($n_{\text{expert}} \geq 250$ for $K{=}2$, $\geq 1{,}000$ for $K{=}4$).

_source line 2491 · 2026-05-22T07:52:33.432679+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We prove that correcting LLM annotation error with a single pooled confusion matrix---standard practice when LLM labels serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis---introduces subgroup-level bias exactly proportional to the product of misclassification heterogeneity and CATE heterogeneity ($\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$), an exact population-level identity verified at $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$).
3	In 50\% of experimental configurations, global correction worsens subgroup CATE bias, with amplification up to 2.86$\times$ ($K{=}2$, difference-in-means) and 5.9$\times$ ($K{=}4$, CausalForest).
4	A natural solution enabled by this insight, Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects (EC-HTE), combines hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified confusion matrix estimation with CausalForestDML, reducing $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.010 ($K{=}2$, extreme $\Delta\pi$) while maintaining nominal coverage ($\geq$0.91 across all configurations) under extreme heterogeneity ($K{=}2$); a computable diagnostic (AUC $= 0.86$) identifies when global correction increases bias from per-subgroup misclassification rates alone, actionable without adopting EC-HTE.
5	Composed with Prediction-Powered Conditional Inference (PPCI) for dual covariate/outcome error, the two corrections are each necessary and jointly sufficient, reducing $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.179 to 0.007.
6	The correction is estimator-agnostic and robust to non-additive treatment effects: replacing CausalForest with the R-learner yields comparable bias reduction (89\%), and a multiplicative DGP yields nominal coverage (0.955).
7	EC-HTE's advantage is conditional on sufficient error heterogeneity; when $\Delta\pi$ is small, global correction suffices.
8	We validate across 520 synthetic configurations (100 Monte Carlo runs each), a semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$), and seven LLMs (four open-source 7B--9B, three proprietary) across two NLP datasets confirming
…[truncated 64 chars]

1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	EC-HTE requires $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ per subgroup for stable hierarchical Bayesian estimation.
4	For $K{=}2$, EC-HTE remains effective even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ (Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}); for $K{=}4$, $P(Z{=}1) \geq 0.3$ is needed to approach breakeven with global correction at $n_{\text{expert}}{=}250$.
5	Even above this threshold, stratification incurs a variance cost: at $K{=}4$, extreme heterogeneity, and $n_{\text{expert}} \leq 100$, CausalForest RMSE reaches 3.5$\times$ that of the naive estimator.
6	The method also requires moderate baseline classifier accuracy per subgroup; when any subgroup's FNR or FPR approaches 0.5, the confusion matrix row approaches a uniform distribution and inversion amplifies noise (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}), as confirmed independently in the hate speech case study (Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}).
7	Additionally, our main semi-synthetic experiments use constant treatment effects across subgroups; a supplementary heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) shows that EC-HTE achieves targeted bias and RMSE improvement in subgroups with large $\Delta\tau$, though aggregate RMSE remains comparable to global correction.
8	Hierarchical pooling of confusion matrices across related subgroups may relax the per-subgroup budget requirement; we leave this extension to future work.
9	
10	The variance-adjusted CausalForest uses a plug-in approximation: it propagates outcome uncertainty through $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ but does not propagate confusion matrix estimation uncertainty itself.
11	This is reasonable at $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 100$ but may understate total uncertainty below this threshold; full Bayesian propagation is left to future work.
12	
13	All main experiments use symmetric error rates and additive CATE injection ($Y(1) = Y(0) + \tau(Z)$).
14	The bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}) generalizes to asymmetric confusion matrices
…[truncated 1375 chars]

```json
{
```
```json
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
```
```json
  "old_string": "Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\\delta_s| / ((1-2\\bar{\\pi}) \\cdot \\pi_s) > 1$, where $\\delta_s = \\pi_s - \\bar{\\pi}$.\nThis condition depends only on per-subgroup misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample, breaking the circular dependency that would arise if the diagnostic required CATE estimates.\nSince the Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} score is a deterministic function of per-subgroup misclassification rates, a threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level for predicting whether global correction increases bias relative to the naive estimator, outperforming the $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ product (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity.",
  "new_string": "Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\\delta_s| / ((1-2\\bar{\\pi}) \\cdot \\pi_s) > 1$, where $\\delta_s = \\pi_s - \\bar{\\pi}$.\nSince this depends only on misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample, it breaks the circular dependency that would arise if CATE estimates were needed.\nA threshold classifier on this score achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level, outperforming the $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ product (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity."
}
```

</details>

当前 Abstract 只突出正面结果（|bias| 0.059→0.010）。

操作：在 Abstract 的结果描述后加一句条件限定：
"When misclassification heterogeneity is moderate and subgroups are unbalanced (e.g., TweetEval), EC-HTE yields comparable bias but higher aggregate RMSE than global correction, indicating that the method's advantage requires sufficient per-subgroup error-rate heterogeneity."

## 修改 3 [Should-Fix] "50%" 措辞

找到 Introduction 或 Experiments 中 "50% of K=2 configurations (260/520)" 出现的所有位置。不要改成模糊措辞。保留 "50%"，加结构化上下文：
改为："global correction worsens CATE bias in 50\% of $K{=}2$ configurations across our parameter grid---concentrated in regimes where $\Delta\pi > 0.10$ and per-subgroup expert samples exceed 25"

## 修改 4 [Minor] 措辞修正

全文搜索 "estimator-agnostic"，改为 "compatible with a range of HTE estimators"。

## 注意
- 编辑后必须确保正文 ≤8 页
- 不要动 theory.tex 的 Remark 2（已经足够了）
- 不要动 limitations.tex（后续根据实验结果可能还需改）
- 编译确认无 critical warnings

1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	\subsection{Setup}
4	
5	We test these predictions on synthetic and semi-synthetic data generating processes (DGPs); full specifications appear in Appendix~\ref{app:dgp}.
6	The synthetic DGP generates $N{=}5{,}000$ observations from a randomized trial with $K \in \{2, 4\}$ subgroups, controlled CATE heterogeneity $\Delta\tau$, and subgroup-specific symmetric misclassification rates with heterogeneity $\Delta\pi$.
7	Expert labels ($n_{\text{expert}} \in \{50, \ldots, 2000\}$) are a simple random sample with true $Z$ observed; each configuration is repeated for 100 Monte Carlo runs.
8	The semi-synthetic DGP samples $N{=}5{,}000$ tweets from TweetEval sentiment~\cite{barbieri2020tweeteval}, defining $K{=}4$ subgroups by crossing hashtag presence and text length (unbalanced: 39\%/38\%/12\%/11\%), with misclassification rates $\pi \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.15, 0.25\}$ reflecting empirical evidence that text features degrade LLM accuracy~\cite{ashwin2025bias}.
9	
10	We compare: (1) \textit{Naive} (no correction), (2) \textit{Global} (pooled confusion matrix, equivalent to PPI/DSL~\cite{angelopoulos2023ppi,egami2024dsl} with homogeneous error), (3) \textit{Stratified MLE} (per-subgroup DSL without shrinkage), (4) \textit{HB EC-HTE} (our method), (5) \textit{PPCI}~\cite{sui2026ppci}, and (6) \textit{EC-HTE+PPCI}.
11	For CausalForest~\cite{wager2018causal}, we compare naive, global, and EC-HTE variants.
12	Primary metrics are $|\text{bias}|$, RMSE, and 95\% CI coverage, averaged across subgroups and 100 MC runs.
13	Robustness validation covers hate speech detection ($\Delta\pi{=}0.117$; Appendix~\ref{app:hatespeech}), six OpenAI models (Appendix~\ref{app:multilm}), subgroup misspecification ($\geq$94.5\% coverage at 50\% noise; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}), and asymmetric confusion matrices (Appendix~\ref{app:semireal}).

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We derived an exact bias identity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\% of $K{=}2$ configurations across our parameter grid.
4	EC-HTE addresses this via hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified correction with a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$).
5	EC-HTE's advantage requires sufficient $\Delta\pi$ and per-subgroup expert budget; in the semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment, moderate heterogeneity and unbalanced subgroups render global correction competitive on aggregate RMSE.
6	The diagnostic criterion (Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) is independently deployable as a pre-analysis check for any study using LLM annotations as effect modifiers.

_source line 2537 · 2026-05-22T16:00:40.847285+00:00_

1	\begin{abstract}
2	We derive an exact bias identity for heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis with LLM-annotated effect modifiers: correcting annotation error with a pooled confusion matrix---standard practice---introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$, the product of each subgroup's misclassification deviation ($\delta_s = \pi_s - \bar{\pi}$) and CATE heterogeneity, verified at $R^2 \geq 0.97$ on both synthetic grids and 11 real LLM confusion matrices.
3	The identity implies that global correction worsens bias in 50\% of $K{=}2$ configurations (260/520) across our parameter grid, with amplification up to 2.86$\times$ (difference-in-means) and 5.9$\times$ ($K{=}4$, CausalForest).
4	Independently, the identity yields a computable diagnostic (AUC $= 0.86$) that flags harmful correction using only per-subgroup misclassification rates---actionable without estimating treatment effects.
5	The identity prescribes per-subgroup correction; we provide a hierarchical Bayesian implementation (EC-HTE) that reduces $|\text{bias}|$ from 0.059 to 0.010 under extreme misclassification heterogeneity ($K{=}2$) with nominal coverage ($\geq$0.91), but whose advantage is conditional: in the semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment (moderate $\Delta\pi$, unbalanced subgroups), global correction achieves lower aggregate RMSE (0.124 vs.\ 0.225).
6	We validate across 520 synthetic configurations (100 Monte Carlo runs each), a semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$), and eight LLMs (open-source 7B--9B and proprietary) across two NLP datasets, confirming both $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity and the bias identity ($R^2 = 0.975$) on real annotations.
7	\end{abstract}

1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:intro}
3	
4	Large language models (LLMs) now routinely replace human annotators in computational social science (CSS), producing labels for sentiment, hate speech, stance, and political discourse at costs orders of magnitude below manual coding~\cite{gilardi2023chatgpt,tornberg2024chatgpt,ziems2024nlp}.
5	Yet these annotations are not uniformly accurate.
6	\citet{ashwin2025bias} document that LLM misclassification rates vary by up to 5$\times$ across demographic subgroups defined by refugee status, gender, and education level.
7	In a pilot study, GPT-4.1 achieves 85.8\% binary accuracy on TweetEval sentiment, but error rates range from 10.2\% to 20.0\% across text-feature subgroups ($\Delta\pi = 0.098$).
8	When such annotations serve as effect modifiers in heterogeneous treatment effect (HTE) analysis, subgroup-specific errors propagate directly into conditional average treatment effect (CATE) estimates.
9	The resulting bias is not random noise that averages out; it is structured by the same demographic axes that define the research question.
10	Standard correction procedures\footnote{Throughout, ``debiasing'' refers to correction of annotation measurement-error bias, not algorithmic fairness debiasing.}, which estimate a single confusion matrix and apply its inverse uniformly, cannot account for this heterogeneity.
11	
12	We formalize this problem and derive an exact bias identity for the correction-induced error.
13	When a pooled confusion matrix is applied to correct subgroup CATEs, it misattributes error across subgroups with an exact product form: the bias in subgroup $s$ equals $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1 - 2\bar{\pi})$, where $\delta_s$ is the deviation from the pooled error rate and $\Delta\tau$ is the CATE heterogeneity (Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity}).
14	This is not a bound but an exact population-level identity, verified empirically with $R^2 = 0.994$ ($K{=}2$) and $R^2 = 0.974$ ($K{=}4$) on synthetic grids, and
…[truncated 4102 chars]

1	\section{EC-HTE Procedure}
2	\label{app:algorithm}
3	
4	\begin{algorithm}[t]
5	\caption{EC-HTE: Error-Corrected Heterogeneous Treatment Effects}
6	\label{alg:echte}
7	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
8	\Require Observations $\{(Y_i, W_i, \hat{Z}_i, X_i)\}_{i=1}^N$; expert labels $\{Z_j^*\}_{j=1}^{n_{\text{expert}}}$; subgroup map $S: \mathcal{X} \to \{1,\ldots,K\}$
9	\Ensure Corrected CATE estimates $\hat{\tau}(z)$ with variance-adjusted CIs
10	\Statex
11	\State \textbf{Preconditions:} verify $n_{\text{expert}} \times P(Z_{\text{minority}}) / K \geq 25$ and $\max_s(\text{FPR}_s, \text{FNR}_s) < 0.5$; if either fails, use global correction
12	\Statex
13	\State \textbf{Step 1: Estimate per-subgroup confusion matrices (HB)}
14	\For{$s = 1, \ldots, K$}
15	    \State $C_s[j,\cdot] \sim \mathrm{Dir}(\alpha_0 \cdot \mathbf{e}_j)$, \; $\alpha_0 \sim \mathrm{Gamma}(3,1)$
16	    \State Gibbs sampler (4 chains, 4000 iter) on $\{(\hat{Z}_j, Z_j^*) : S_j = s\}$
17	    \State $\hat{C}_s \gets$ posterior mean; verify $\hat{R} < 1.01$
18	\EndFor
19	\Statex
20	\State \textbf{Step 2: Correct observed outcome moments}
21	\For{$s = 1, \ldots, K$}
22	    \If{$\kappa(\hat{C}_s) > 100$} $\hat{C}_s \gets \bar{C}$ \Comment{singularity guard}
23	    \EndIf
24	    \State $\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s \gets \hat{C}_s^{-1} \hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_s$
25	\EndFor
26	\Statex
27	\State \textbf{Step 3: CausalForestDML with variance-adjusted SEs}
28	\State Train CausalForest (honest splitting) on corrected labels
29	\State $\mathrm{se}_{\mathrm{adj},i} \gets \sqrt{\mathrm{diag}(\hat{C}_{S_i}^{-1}\, \mathrm{diag}(\mathrm{se}_i^2)\, \hat{C}_{S_i}^{-\top})}$
30	\State \Return $\hat{\tau}(z)$ with 95\% CIs from $\mathrm{se}_{\mathrm{adj}}$
31	\end{algorithmic}
32	\end{algorithm}
33

35	\label{app:derivation}
36	
37	\paragraph{Setup.}
38	Under assumptions (A1)--(A4), the observed CATE in subgroup $s$ at effect-modifier level $z$ is
39	\begin{equation}

492	All six models exhibit effective $\Delta\pi > 0.05$ on the four retained subgroups (Table~\ref{tab:multilm}), and 5 of 6 share the same worst subgroup (\texttt{True\_medium}: tweets with hashtags and medium text length).
493	Newer models reduce absolute error rates but do not eliminate subgroup heterogeneity: GPT-5.4 achieves $\Delta\pi = 0.114$ compared to GPT-3.5-Turbo's $0.198$.
494	These results were obtained with a single API provider; open-source model validation appears in Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}.

496	\section{Open-Source Multi-LLM Validation}
497	\label{app:multilm_opensource}
498	
499	We evaluate four open-source LLMs (7B--9B parameter range) on 490 TweetEval sentiment tweets with $K{=}4$ subgroups defined by crossing hashtag presence and text length.
500	Table~\ref{tab:multilm_cm} reports the confusion matrix heterogeneity profile.
501	All four models exhibit $\Delta\pi > 0.05$, confirming that subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification is not specific to proprietary APIs.
502	Subgroup error rankings are consistent across architectures: \texttt{True\_short} (short tweets with hashtags) has the lowest $\pi_s$ in 3 of 4 models, while \texttt{False\_medium} (medium tweets without hashtags) has the highest.

_source line 2560 · 2026-05-22T16:03:24.195113+00:00_

28	\citet{mozer2026ppi} extend PPI to arm-specific ATE correction; PPCI~\cite{sui2026ppci} targets conditional estimands but corrects outcome-side error ($\hat{Y}$ given true $Z$), not covariate-side misclassification; ForestIV~\cite{bu2023forestiv} targets the outcome model rather than the heterogeneity structure.
29	None corrects bias when the mismeasured variable is the effect modifier indexing the CATE function.
30	\citet{imai2010causal} study treatment misclassification, requiring instrumental variables or partial identification; our problem is structurally distinct because the treatment is observed correctly but the effect modifier is misclassified, admitting exact correction through confusion matrices rather than bounds.
31	Extended discussion of these distinctions appears in Appendix~\ref{app:related}.
32	Our validation combines synthetic DGPs, semi-synthetic experiments, and real LLM confusion matrices, consistent with PPI and DSL evaluation methodology; Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource} validates both $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity and the bias identity itself ($R^2 = 0.975$) across 11 model--dataset combinations.
33

825	Subgroups are defined by \texttt{text\_length\_bin} $\times$ \texttt{has\_question} ($K{=}4$): S0 (long, no question, 38.8\%), S1 (long, question, 11.4\%), S2 (short, no question, 33.7\%), S3 (short, question, 16.1\%).
826	Subgroup features were chosen a priori based on observable text properties; no feature search was performed.
827	Three proprietary LLMs annotate each sample via an OpenAI-compatible API, yielding 800--900 valid annotations per model.
828	
829	Table~\ref{tab:civil_cm} reports per-subgroup FPR and FNR.
830	We define $\Delta\pi = \max_s \pi_s - \min_s \pi_s$, where $\pi_s$ is the overall misclassification rate for subgroup $s$.
831	All three models exhibit $\Delta\pi > 0.05$, with overall heterogeneity $\Delta\pi \in [0.113, 0.160]$---comparable to the $\Delta\pi \in [0.125, 0.165]$ observed across four open-source LLMs on TweetEval (Section~\ref{sec:multilm_opensource}).
832	GPT-4o-mini and GPT-3.5-turbo show pronounced FPR/FNR asymmetry (e.g., GPT-4o-mini FPR $\in [0.42, 0.61]$ vs.\ FNR $\in [0.05, 0.16]$), indicating systematic over-prediction of toxicity; their FPR approaches 0.5, placing them in the ill-conditioned regime where confusion matrix inversion amplifies noise (consistent with the classifier quality threshold discussed in the Limitations section).
833	The consistent presence of $\Delta\pi$ heterogeneity across two NLP tasks, seven LLMs spanning open-source (7B--9B) and proprietary families, and two distinct subgroup definitions confirms that this phenomenon is a structural property of LLM annotation rather than an artifact of a specific dataset or model.

```json
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Multi-LLM Validation}\n\\label{sec:multilm_opensource}\n\nTo verify generalization, we annotate TweetEval tweets with four open-source LLMs (7B--9B; Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm_opensource}) and Civil Comments~\\cite{borkan2019nuanced} with three proprietary LLMs (Appendix~\\ref{app:civilcomments}).\nAll seven models exhibit $\\Delta\\pi \\in [0.113, 0.165]$ ($K{=}4$), confirming subgroup-heterogeneous misclassification as a structural property of LLM annotation across model families and datasets.\nRegressing observed subgroup bias on the Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity} prediction using each model's real (asymmetric) confusion matrix yields $R^2 = 0.975$ across all 11 model--dataset combinations (per-model range: $0.93$--$0.99$; Appendix~\\ref{app:realcm_bias}), extending the identity from synthetic to real annotation error structures.",
  "new_string": "We further validate the bias identity across eight LLMs spanning 11 model--dataset combinations ($R^2 = 0.975$; Appendix~\\ref{app:multilm})."
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "We derived an exact bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\\% of $K{=}2$ configurations across our parameter grid.\nEC-HTE addresses this via hierarchical Bayesian subgroup-stratified correction with a $\\Delta\\tau$-free diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$).\nEC-HTE's advantage requires sufficient $\\Delta\\pi$ and per-subgroup expert budget; in the semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment, moderate heterogeneity and unbalanced subgroups render global correction competitive on aggregate RMSE.\nThe diagnostic criterion (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}) is independently deployable as a pre-analysis check for any study using LLM annotations as effect modifiers.",
  "new_string": "We derived an exact bias identity (Theorem~\\ref{thm:bias_identity}): global confusion-matrix correction introduces subgroup-level bias proportional to $\\delta_s \\cdot \\Delta\\tau$, worsening CATE estimates in 50\\% of $K{=}2$ configurations.\nEC-HTE provides subgroup-stratified correction via hierarchical Bayes; the companion diagnostic (Proposition~\\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts}; AUC $= 0.86$) flags harmful correction using only per-subgroup misclassification rates.\nEC-HTE's advantage requires sufficient $\\Delta\\pi$ and per-subgroup expert budget; when either is marginal, global correction is competitive."
}
```

_source line 2593 · 2026-05-22T16:06:36.539033+00:00_

82	\subsection{Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE}
83	\label{sec:diagnostic}
84	
85	Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, where $\delta_s = \pi_s - \bar{\pi}$.
86	Since this depends only on misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample, practitioners can flag harmful correction before estimating any treatment effects.
87	A threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (0.89 at the setting level), outperforming $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity.
88	An empirical CV($\pi_s$) achieves higher setting-level AUC (0.97) but lacks theoretical grounding and is sensitive to $K$; Figure~\ref{fig:roc} (Appendix) shows the ROC curve.
89	
90	\paragraph{Recommended workflow.}
91	Practitioners should (i) collect $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 25K / P(Z_{\text{minority}})$ stratified expert labels, (ii) verify per-subgroup budget $\geq 25$, $\max(\text{FPR}, \text{FNR}) < 0.5$, and $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) \leq 100$ (Section~\ref{sec:method}), (iii) evaluate the diagnostic (Equation~\ref{eq:hurts_condition}) to determine whether global correction is harmful, and (iv) apply EC-HTE when the diagnostic flags any subgroup; PPCI can be composed for outcome error (Appendix~\ref{sec:ppci}).
92	Expert budget crossover curves and the applicability frontier appear in Appendices~\ref{sec:budget} and~\ref{app:semireal}.
93	Appendix~\ref{app:screening} applies the diagnostic to 11 real LLM confusion matrices, and Appendix~\ref{app:budget_sensitivity} quantifies the per-subgroup expert budget threshold below which global correction is preferred.
94	
95	We further validate the bias identity across eight LLMs spanning 11 model--dataset combinations ($R^2 = 0.975$; Appendix~\ref{app:multilm}).

```json
{
```
```json
  "replace_all": "False",
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520/projects/ec_hte_llm_annotation/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
```
```json
  "old_string": "In the semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K{=}4$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$), EC-HTE and Global achieve comparable $|\\text{bias}|$ (0.027 vs.\\ 0.023, $p{=}0.395$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}), but EC-HTE incurs higher aggregate RMSE (0.225 vs.\\ 0.124).\nThree factors explain this result.\nFirst, TweetEval's $\\Delta\\pi{=}0.20$ is moderate, yielding small absolute $\\delta_s$ values and limited bias-reduction headroom for subgroup-specific correction.\nSecond, the subgroups are highly unbalanced (S0: 39\\%, S1: 38\\%, S2: 12\\%, S3: 11\\%): the two small subgroups receive only ${\\sim}30$ expert labels each at $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$, barely above the per-subgroup budget threshold.\nThird, per-subgroup analysis reveals stark RMSE heterogeneity: EC-HTE matches Global in the large subgroups (S0--S1; RMSE ratio ${\\approx}1.0$) but degrades in S3 (RMSE 0.480 vs.\\ 0.145, a 3.3$\\times$ penalty), where noisy $\\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ estimation dominates the aggregate.\nEC-HTE's advantage thus requires both sufficient $\\Delta\\pi$ and adequate per-subgroup expert budget ($n_{\\text{expert}} \\cdot P(Z_{\\text{minority}}) / K \\geq 25$; Section~\\ref{sec:diagnostic}); when either is marginal, as in TweetEval's moderate-$\\Delta\\pi$, unbalanced-subgroup regime, global correction is competitive or superior on aggregate RMSE.",
  "new_string": "In the semi-synthetic TweetEval experiment ($N{=}5{,}000$, $K{=}4$, $n_{\\text{expert}}{=}250$), EC-HTE and Global achieve comparable $|\\text{bias}|$ (0.027 vs.\\ 0.023, $p{=}0.395$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_supp}), but EC-HTE incurs higher aggregate RMSE (0.225 vs.\\ 0.124), driven by moderate $\\Delta\\pi{=}0.20$, highly unbalanced subgroups (12\\%/11\\% receiving ${\\sim}30$ expert labels each), and RMSE degradation in small subgroups where noisy $\\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ estimation dominates (S3: 0.480 vs.\\ 0.145).\nEC-HTE's advantage requires both sufficient $\\Delta\\pi$ and adequate per-subgroup expert budget; when either is marginal, global correction is competitive on aggregate RMSE."
}
```

[Director 指令] [Patch] Round 11 修复第二批——4 项。不用验证引用。

## 修改 1 [Must-Fix] Section 5.4 诊断段落加 K=2 caveat

experiments.tex Section 5.4 (Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE) 当前报告 AUC=0.86。需要在该段落末尾（在 "Recommended workflow" 之前）加一段 honest disclosure：

"On real LLM confusion matrices (16 model--dataset combinations, Appendix~\ref{app:screening}), the pooled diagnostic AUC decreases to 0.63, primarily because real classifiers exhibit asymmetric FPR/FNR ratios (e.g., 7:1) that violate~(A2). For $K{=}2$ subgroups, $|\delta_0|{=}|\delta_1|$ causes the diagnostic to rank subgroups solely by $1/\pi_s$, which can anti-predict actual harm. The diagnostic is most reliable for $K{\geq}4$ with moderate error-rate asymmetry."

## 修改 2 [Should-Fix] Missing References

在 references.bib 添加两条，并在 related_work.tex 适当位置引用：

1. Semenova & Chernozhukov (2021) "Debiased machine learning of conditional average treatment effects" — Econometrica. 在 Related Work 讨论 CATE estimation 的段落引用。

2. Blackwell, Honaker, and King (2017) "A Unified Approach to Measurement Error and Missing Data" — Political Analysis. 在 Related Work 讨论 measurement error 的段落引用。

注意：这两条引用必须是真实的，请用 WebSearch 确认准确的 journal、volume、pages 信息。如果无法确认，不要添加（宁可不引也不要假引用）。

## 修改 3 [Should-Fix] W6: (A3) 讨论扩展

theory.tex 中 (A3) 讨论（约 L68 附近）当前只有 1 句。在其后加 2 句：
"Empirical verification under class imbalance (Appendix~\ref{app:classimbalance}) shows that the bias identity maintains $R^2 \geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ for $K{=}2$, though EC-HTE's RMSE ratio relative to global correction increases to 1.06--1.68 as class imbalance grows. In practice, NLP classification tasks often exhibit substantial class imbalance (e.g., hate speech prevalence $\ll 50\%$), and practitioners should verify that the per-subgroup expert budget threshold remains satisfied under the actual class distribution."

## 修改 4 [Should-Fix] W7: Related Work F-L 区分强化

在 related_work.tex 中讨论 Frazis & Loewenstein (2003) 的段落，加强 formal comparison。核心论点：
- F-L 刻画**未校正**估计量的同质衰减：β̃ = β(1-π₀-π₁)
- Theorem 1 刻画**校正后**估计量在子群异质性下的偏差放大：bias(z,s) = δ_s·Δτ/(1-2π̄)
- F-L 是 K=1（无子群异质性）的退化情况：δ_s=0 → 校正后无额外偏差
- 代数工具不同：OLS 衰减 vs 混淆矩阵求逆
- 核心洞察不同："未校正有衰减" vs "校正本身在异质性下引入方向可变的新偏差"

用 3-4 句话写清楚，不要写成对比表。

## 编译

78	\subsection{Actionable Diagnostic Without EC-HTE}
79	\label{sec:diagnostic}
80	
81	Proposition~\ref{prop:debiasing_hurts} provides a $\Delta\tau$-free diagnostic: global correction increases bias in subgroup $s$ if and only if $|\delta_s| / ((1-2\bar{\pi}) \cdot \pi_s) > 1$, where $\delta_s = \pi_s - \bar{\pi}$.
82	Since this depends only on misclassification rates estimable from a pilot sample, practitioners can flag harmful correction before estimating any treatment effects.
83	A threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (0.89 at the setting level), outperforming $\Delta\pi \times \Delta\tau$ (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity (Figure~\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix).
84	
85	\paragraph{Recommended workflow.}
86	Practitioners should (i) collect $n_{\text{expert}} \geq 25K / P(Z_{\text{minority}})$ stratified expert labels, (ii) verify per-subgroup budget $\geq 25$, $\max(\text{FPR}, \text{FNR}) < 0.5$, and $\kappa(\hat{C}_s) \leq 100$ (Section~\ref{sec:method}), (iii) evaluate the diagnostic (Equation~\ref{eq:hurts_condition}), and (iv) apply EC-HTE when the diagnostic flags any subgroup; PPCI can be composed for outcome error (Appendix~\ref{sec:ppci}).
87	Budget crossover curves, applicability frontier, and diagnostic screening of 11 real LLM confusion matrices appear in Appendices~\ref{sec:budget},~\ref{app:semireal}, and~\ref{app:screening}.
88	
89	We further validate the bias identity across eight LLMs spanning 11 model--dataset combinations ($R^2 = 0.975$; Appendix~\ref{app:multilm}).

62	Assumption~(A5) simplifies exposition; the identity generalizes to subgroup-specific $\Delta\tau_s$: when treatment effects vary across subgroups, the bias in subgroup $s$ becomes $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau_s / (1 - 2\bar{\pi})$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:a5_relaxation} in Appendix~\ref{app:a5_violation}, verified empirically in the heterogeneous-$\tau$ DGP of Appendix~\ref{app:multilm_opensource}).
63	Critically, EC-HTE's correction mechanism---per-subgroup confusion matrix inversion---does not depend on (A5): each $\hat{C}_s^{-1}$ corrects the local misclassification structure regardless of whether $\Delta\tau$ varies across subgroups.
64	Appendix~\ref{app:a5_violation} verifies this empirically under both positive and negative $\Delta\tau_s$--$\delta_s$ correlation; EC-HTE maintains $\geq$93\% coverage while global correction degrades to 74--82\%.
65	
66	The identity extends to $K > 2$ subgroups with $J$ effect-modifier levels; the multi-class bias retains the same product structure with modified amplification terms (Corollary~\ref{cor:multiclass} in Appendix~\ref{app:multiclass}).

1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Measurement error and annotation models.}
5	Classical measurement error theory shows that correction via estimated error models can amplify MSE when the error structure is misspecified~\cite{fuller1987measurement,carroll2006measurement}; Bayesian approaches require informative priors when identification is weak~\cite{gustafson2003measurement}, and nonparametric methods use repeated measurements or instruments to recover the true distribution~\cite{hu2008nonparametric}.
6	
7	Three distinctions separate our contribution from prior measurement error analyses.
8	First, we study effect modifier misclassification---the mismeasured variable defines the CATE function itself---rather than regressor~\cite{frazis2003estimating,carroll2006measurement} or confounder~\cite{ogburn2012nondifferential,greenland1980effect} error, where attenuation is the primary concern.
9	Second, Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} derives an exact identity for correction-induced bias---bias created by the debiasing procedure---rather than bounding the attenuation from misclassification itself.
10	Concretely, \citet{frazis2003estimating} derive scalar attenuation $\tilde{\beta} = \beta(1 - \pi_0 - \pi_1)$ for the uncorrected estimator under homogeneous misclassification; Theorem~\ref{thm:bias_identity} characterizes a structurally distinct quantity---correction-induced bias $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau / (1 - 2\bar{\pi})$---that is subgroup-specific, potentially sign-reversing, and absent from the uncorrected estimator.
11	Third, the $\delta_s \cdot \Delta\tau$ product structure arises only under heterogeneous error rates across subgroups; all homogeneous-error analyses~\cite{battistin2014treatment,fong2021machine} miss this interaction entirely.
12	In the annotation literature, \citet{dawid1979maximum} introduced confusion matrix estimation for multiple annotators, with extensions to item difficulty, annotator competence, and hierarchical pri

## Reference 1: Semenova & Chernozhukov (2021)

**Status: EXISTS — but the journal is The Econometrics Journal, NOT Econometrica.**

These are different journals. *Econometrica* is published by the Econometric Society (Wiley); *The Econometrics Journal* is published by Oxford University Press for the Royal Economic Society. The claim that this paper appeared in *Econometrica* is **incorrect**.

- **Title:** Debiased Machine Learning of Conditional Average Treatment Effects and Other Causal Functions
- **Authors:** Vira Semenova, Victor Chernozhukov
- **Journal:** *The Econometrics Journal* (Oxford University Press)
- **Volume/Issue:** 24(2)
- **Pages:** 264–289
- **Year:** 2021
- **DOI:** `10.1093/ectj/utaa027`

```json
  "old_string": "A threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (0.89 at the setting level), outperforming $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix).\n\n\\paragraph{Recommended workflow.}",
  "new_string": "A threshold classifier achieves AUC = 0.86 at the subgroup level (0.89 at the setting level), outperforming $\\Delta\\pi \\times \\Delta\\tau$ (AUC = 0.73) which requires unobservable CATE heterogeneity (Figure~\\ref{fig:roc}, Appendix).\nOn real LLM confusion matrices (16 model--dataset combinations, Appendix~\\ref{app:screening}), the pooled diagnostic AUC decreases to 0.63, primarily because real classifiers exhibit asymmetric FPR/FNR ratios (e.g., 7:1) that violate~(A2).\nFor $K{=}2$ subgroups, $|\\delta_0|{=}|\\delta_1|$ causes the diagnostic to rank subgroups solely by $1/\\pi_s$, which can anti-predict actual harm.\nThe diagnostic is most reliable for $K{\\geq}4$ with moderate error-rate asymmetry.\n\n\\paragraph{Recommended workflow.}"
}
```

```json
  "old_string": "Assumption~(A3) ensures $M_s = C_s$; when $P(Z{=}1) \\neq 0.5$, the mixing matrices acquire asymmetric entries, but the product structure persists with modified coefficients (Appendix~\\ref{app:derivation}).\nEmpirical verification (Section~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}) yields $R^2 = 0.994$ for $K{=}2$ and $R^2 = 0.974$ for $K{=}4$; the residual variance reflects finite-sample Monte Carlo noise, not model misspecification.",
  "new_string": "Assumption~(A3) ensures $M_s = C_s$; when $P(Z{=}1) \\neq 0.5$, the mixing matrices acquire asymmetric entries, but the product structure persists with modified coefficients (Appendix~\\ref{app:derivation}).\nEmpirical verification under class imbalance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classimbalance}) shows that the bias identity maintains $R^2 \\geq 0.976$ even at $P(Z{=}1) = 0.1$ for $K{=}2$, though EC-HTE's RMSE ratio relative to global correction increases to 1.06--1.68 as class imbalance grows.\nIn practice, NLP classification tasks often exhibit substantial class imbalance (e.g., hate speech prevalence $\\ll 50\\%$), and practitioners should verify that the per-subgroup expert budget threshold remains satisfied under the actual class distribution.\nEmpirical verification (Section~\\ref{sec:bias_mechanism}) yields $R^2 = 0.994$ for $K{=}2$ and $R^2 = 0.974$ for $K{=}4$; the residual variance reflects finite-sample Monte Carlo noise, not model misspecification."
}
```
